I mean, I might shift around if something’s long, but I haven’t noticed feeling unusually restless or like I can’t sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any situations in the past few weeks where you had trouble staying focused, such as during conversations, while driving, or in other activities that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly when I’m trying to read or write at my desk. Like, a siren outside or someone talking in the hallway can pull me off, or even just some random thought about an exhibit idea. Then I’ll realize I’ve kind of lost twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself starting new tasks before finishing old ones, even if it’s not related to being overly excited or impulsive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I’ll start one thing, like sorting rent papers or making notes for a gallery idea, and then end up opening article PDFs or making another to-do list before I’ve finished the first thing. My laptop and apartment kind of show that. It’s mostly hard to tell what should actually come first.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or tasks where you find it hard to focus, even if they’re important or require a lot of attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… bigger reading or writing stuff, mostly. If I know it’s going to take real concentration, like a curatorial proposal or a dense theory chapter, I’ll kind of… do smaller errands first. Then it ends up being the night before, and I still haven’t really started.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to organize tasks or activities, or avoid things that require sustained mental effort, like planning an event or working on a long project?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is hard. My apartment and laptop both kind of turn into these half-started things—rent papers, gallery notes, PDFs, to-do lists. I can start making a plan, but figuring out what actually has to happen first is where I get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself feeling internally restless, like your mind is constantly racing, even if you’re not physically moving around a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my mind can feel busy sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it racing. I haven’t noticed that as a main thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… mostly it’s that I keep making small mistakes in things I turn in. Like applications or research notes. I’ll think I checked them, but then there’s some embarrassing thing I missed, like a wrong artwork date or a skipped question. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused on tasks or notice that your attention gets easily pulled away by distractions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. If I’m at my desk trying to read or write, little things pull me off pretty easily — like a siren outside, people talking in the hallway, or even just some random thought about an exhibit idea. Then I’ll realize I’ve been off track for, like, twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting on impulse without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, I might fidget a little sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m always restless or acting without thinking. That’s not really what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, or feeling like you’re not doing enough or not good enough lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I get frustrated with myself when I mess things up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things or felt really down lately.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say these kinds of attention issues started when you were a child, or do they seem more like something that developed later on?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back pretty far. Like, even in elementary school, teachers would say I had good ideas, but I’d forget to turn in worksheets or lose the instructions. Little details got missed unless my parents checked my backpack every night.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these attention issues in other parts of your life, like at home or in social situations, or do they mostly happen when you're working or studying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it happens outside school too. Like at home I’ll mean to do basic stuff—return an email, pay a bill, pick up groceries—and then a few days later I realize I just… never did it. It kind of drops out of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself doing things without thinking them through, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or starting new tasks before finishing old ones?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. Not so much blurting answers, but in seminars or gallery discussions I can get too into explaining an idea and go on longer than I meant to. Then afterward I realize I didn’t really leave much room for anyone else to say anything.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when this difficulty with follow-through or staying focused happened in a setting that wasn’t work or home—like in a social situation or somewhere public?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not a big example, but sometimes if I’m at a library or a café trying to read or write, something small—like a siren outside, or people talking nearby—pulls me off. Then I’m just thinking about something else, like an exhibit idea, and I don’t realize I’ve lost twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any situations where you felt unusually restless or fidgety, especially in places where you're expected to stay still, like during meetings or in public transportation?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might shift around if something’s long, but I haven’t noticed feeling unusually restless or like I can’t sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any situations in the past few weeks where you had trouble staying focused, such as during conversations, while driving, or in other activities that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly when I’m trying to read or write at my desk. Like, a siren outside or someone talking in the hallway can pull me off, or even just some random thought about an exhibit idea. Then I’ll realize I’ve kind of lost twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself starting new tasks before finishing old ones, even if it’s not related to being overly excited or impulsive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I’ll start one thing, like sorting rent papers or making notes for a gallery idea, and then end up opening article PDFs or making another to-do list before I’ve finished the first thing. My laptop and apartment kind of show that. It’s mostly hard to tell what should actually come first.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or tasks where you find it hard to focus, even if they’re important or require a lot of attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… bigger reading or writing stuff, mostly. If I know it’s going to take real concentration, like a curatorial proposal or a dense theory chapter, I’ll kind of… do smaller errands first. Then it ends up being the night before, and I still haven’t really started.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to organize tasks or activities, or avoid things that require sustained mental effort, like planning an event or working on a long project?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is hard. My apartment and laptop both kind of turn into these half-started things—rent papers, gallery notes, PDFs, to-do lists. I can start making a plan, but figuring out what actually has to happen first is where I get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself feeling internally restless, like your mind is constantly racing, even if you’re not physically moving around a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my mind can feel busy sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it racing. I haven’t noticed that as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or lost interest in activities, even things you usually enjoy, for most of the day, more days than not?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I have off days, but I haven’t noticed it being most of the day or happening more days than not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t enjoy things, even things you usually like, for a period of time—even if it was just a few days?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, maybe for a day here or there I’m not really into anything, but I wouldn’t say I couldn’t enjoy things for a few days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stay focused on tasks or notice that your attention gets easily pulled away by distractions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. If I’m at my desk trying to read or write, little things pull me off pretty easily — like a siren outside, people talking in the hallway, or even just some random thought about an exhibit idea. Then I’ll realize I’ve been off track for, like, twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless, have trouble sitting still, or find yourself acting on impulse without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think. I mean, I might fidget a little sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m always restless or acting without thinking. That’s not really what I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, or feeling like you’re not doing enough or not good enough lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I get frustrated with myself when I mess things up, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things or felt really down lately.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say these kinds of attention issues started when you were a child, or do they seem more like something that developed later on?\n\nPatient: I think it goes back pretty far. Like, even in elementary school, teachers would say I had good ideas, but I’d forget to turn in worksheets or lose the instructions. Little details got missed unless my parents checked my backpack every night.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these attention issues in other parts of your life, like at home or in social situations, or do they mostly happen when you're working or studying?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it happens outside school too. Like at home I’ll mean to do basic stuff—return an email, pay a bill, pick up groceries—and then a few days later I realize I just… never did it. It kind of drops out of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself doing things without thinking them through, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or starting new tasks before finishing old ones?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. Not so much blurting answers, but in seminars or gallery discussions I can get too into explaining an idea and go on longer than I meant to. Then afterward I realize I didn’t really leave much room for anyone else to say anything.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when this difficulty with follow-through or staying focused happened in a setting that wasn’t work or home—like in a social situation or somewhere public?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not a big example, but sometimes if I’m at a library or a café trying to read or write, something small—like a siren outside, or people talking nearby—pulls me off. Then I’m just thinking about something else, like an exhibit idea, and I don’t realize I’ve lost twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any situations where you felt unusually restless or fidgety, especially in places where you're expected to stay still, like during meetings or in public transportation?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might shift around if something’s long, but I haven’t noticed feeling unusually restless or like I can’t sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any situations in the past few weeks where you had trouble staying focused, such as during conversations, while driving, or in other activities that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly when I’m trying to read or write at my desk. Like, a siren outside or someone talking in the hallway can pull me off, or even just some random thought about an exhibit idea. Then I’ll realize I’ve kind of lost twenty minutes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself starting new tasks before finishing old ones, even if it’s not related to being overly excited or impulsive?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. I’ll start one thing, like sorting rent papers or making notes for a gallery idea, and then end up opening article PDFs or making another to-do list before I’ve finished the first thing. My laptop and apartment kind of show that. It’s mostly hard to tell what should actually come first.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or tasks where you find it hard to focus, even if they’re important or require a lot of attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah… bigger reading or writing stuff, mostly. If I know it’s going to take real concentration, like a curatorial proposal or a dense theory chapter, I’ll kind of… do smaller errands first. Then it ends up being the night before, and I still haven’t really started.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to organize tasks or activities, or avoid things that require sustained mental effort, like planning an event or working on a long project?\n\nPatient: Yeah… organizing is hard. My apartment and laptop both kind of turn into these half-started things—rent papers, gallery notes, PDFs, to-do lists. I can start making a plan, but figuring out what actually has to happen first is where I get stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself feeling internally restless, like your mind is constantly racing, even if you’re not physically moving around a lot?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, my mind can feel busy sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it racing. I haven’t noticed that as a main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or lost interest in activities, even things you usually enjoy, for most of the day, more days than not?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I have off days, but I haven’t noticed it being most of the day or happening more days than not.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you couldn’t enjoy things, even things you usually like, for a period of time—even if it was just a few days?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, maybe for a day here or there I’m not really into anything, but I wouldn’t say I couldn’t enjoy things for a few days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of time lasting at least two weeks where most days you felt down or couldn’t enjoy things, even things you usually like?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for like two weeks or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had times when you felt down or weren’t able to enjoy things, even things you usually like, for most of the day, nearly every day for a week or more?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s been like that. Maybe a rough day or two, but not most of the day nearly every day for a week.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
